LAB DATASET: LONG TEXT CORPUS FOR CHUNKING AND EMBEDDING
Dataset ID: LAB-AI-2026-001
Prepared by: Engr. Kamran Yousaf
Organization: Pakistan Institute of Data Engineering
Created: 2026-11-08
Encoding: UTF-8

INSTRUCTIONS
Use this file to test text loading, line splitting, chunk overlap, embedding generation,
vector indexing, semantic search, and metadata extraction. All people and organizations
in this dataset are fictional test entities.

RECORD 0001
Record title: Retrieval-Augmented Generation — practical lab entry 1
Record owner: Amina Rahman
Department: Open Knowledge Lab
Office location: Lahore, Pakistan
Primary technology: FAISS
Related technology: PostgreSQL
Reference code: LAB-2026-0001
Recorded date: 2026-02-02

Description:
RAG combines retrieval with language generation. A retriever finds relevant passages, and a generator uses those passages to produce a grounded response.
Amina Rahman is coordinating a controlled experiment at Open Knowledge Lab. The experiment evaluates retrieval-augmented generation with FAISS. The supporting prototype uses PostgreSQL for one part of the workflow. The record is deliberately written with repeated entities so that tests can measure whether an embedding model groups related passages and whether metadata extraction can identify people, organizations, places, dates, and technical terms.

Procedure:
1. Load the record without changing its original wording.
2. Split the document using the chunking strategy under evaluation.
3. Attach the record code, title, owner, and date to every resulting chunk.
4. Generate embeddings using the configured embedding model.
5. Store vectors and metadata in the selected vector database.
6. Search using both a direct keyword query and a paraphrased query.
7. Compare the returned record codes with the expected relevant records.

Expected observations:
A query about retrieval-augmented generation should retrieve this record or another relevant record. A query containing the name Amina Rahman may retrieve records owned by the same person. A query about Lahore should be supported by location metadata. Exact searches for FAISS and PostgreSQL should remain possible even when semantic similarity is used.

Quality checks:
- No text should disappear during file loading.
- Chunk boundaries should not break every sentence into isolated fragments.
- Each chunk should retain a stable source record code.
- Similarity scores should be stored with the retrieved chunk identifiers.
- The system should not treat fictional examples as verified real-world facts.

End of record.

========================================================================

RECORD 0002
Record title: Text Chunking — practical lab entry 2
Record owner: Bilal Ahmed
Department: Data Systems Group
Office location: Islamabad, Pakistan
Primary technology: LangChain
Related technology: Python
Reference code: LAB-2026-0002
Recorded date: 2026-03-03

Description:
Chunking divides long documents into smaller passages while preserving enough context for retrieval. Fixed-size, sentence-aware, and semantic strategies make different trade-offs.
Bilal Ahmed is coordinating a controlled experiment at Data Systems Group. The experiment evaluates text chunking with LangChain. The supporting prototype uses Python for one part of the workflow. The record is deliberately written with repeated entities so that tests can measure whether an embedding model groups related passages and whether metadata extraction can identify people, organizations, places, dates, and technical terms.

3. Attach the record code, title, owner, and date to every resulting chunk.
4. Generate embeddings using the configured embedding model.
5. Store vectors and metadata in the selected vector database.
6. Search using both a direct keyword query and a paraphrased query.
7. Compare the returned record codes with the expected relevant records.

Expected observations:
A query about text chunking should retrieve this record or another relevant record. A query containing the name Bilal Ahmed may retrieve records owned by the same person. A query about Islamabad should be supported by location metadata. Exact searches for LangChain and Python should remain possible even when semantic similarity is used.

Quality checks:
- No text should disappear during file loading.
- Chunk boundaries should not break every sentence into isolated fragments.
- Each chunk should retain a stable source record code.
- Similarity scores should be stored with the retrieved chunk identifiers.
- The system should not treat fictional examples as verified real-world facts.

Office location: Karachi, Pakistan
Primary technology: Sentence Transformers
Related technology: PyTorch
Reference code: LAB-2026-0003
Recorded date: 2026-04-04

Reference code: LAB-2026-0035
Recorded date: 2026-12-09

Description:
An embedding model maps text into a numerical vector space. Similar meanings may occupy nearby regions, enabling semantic search beyond exact keyword matching.
Sara Malik is coordinating a controlled experiment at Applied AI Centre. The experiment evaluates vector embeddings with Sentence Transformers. The supporting prototype uses PyTorch for one part of the workflow. The record is deliberately written with repeated entities so that tests can measure whether an embedding model groups related passages and whether metadata extraction can identify people, organizations, places, dates, and technical terms.

Procedure:
1. Load the record without changing its original wording.
2. Split the document using the chunking strategy under evaluation.
3. Attach the record code, title, owner, and date to every resulting chunk.
4. Generate embeddings using the configured embedding model.
5. Store vectors and metadata in the selected vector database.
6. Search using both a direct keyword query and a paraphrased query.
7. Compare the returned record codes with the expected relevant records.

Expected observations:
A query about vector embeddings should retrieve this record or another relevant record. A query containing the name Sara Malik may retrieve records owned by the same person. A query about Karachi should be supported by location metadata. Exact searches for Sentence Transformers and PyTorch should remain possible even when semantic similarity is used.

Quality checks:
- No text should disappear during file loading.
- Chunk boundaries should not break every sentence into isolated fragments.
- Each chunk should retain a stable source record code.
- Similarity scores should be stored with the retrieved chunk identifiers.
- The system should not treat fictional examples as verified real-world facts.

End of record.

The supporting prototype uses JSON for one part of the workflow. The record is deliberately written with repeated entities so that tests can measure whether an embedding model groups related passages and whether metadata extraction can identify people, organizations, places, dates, and technical terms.

Procedure:
1. Load the record without changing its original wording.
2. Split the document using the chunking strategy under evaluation.
3. Attach the record code, title, owner, and date to every resulting chunk.
4. Generate embeddings using the configured embedding model.
5. Store vectors and metadata in the selected vector database.
6. Search using both a direct keyword query and a paraphrased query.
7. Compare the returned record codes with the expected relevant records.

Expected observations:
A query about metadata filtering should retrieve this record or another relevant record. A query containing the name Hamza Qureshi may retrieve records owned by the same person. A query about Peshawar should be supported by location metadata. Exact searches for Qdrant and JSON should remain possible even when semantic similarity is used.

Quality checks:
- No text should disappear during file loading.
- Chunk boundaries should not break every sentence into isolated fragments.
- Each chunk should retain a stable source record code.
- Similarity scores should be stored with the retrieved chunk identifiers.
- The system should not treat fictional examples as verified real-world facts.

Record title: Metadata Filtering — practical lab entry 172
Record owner: Hamza Qureshi
Department: Digital Library Unit
Office location: Peshawar, Pakistan
Primary technology: Qdrant
Related technology: JSON
Reference code: LAB-2026-0172
Recorded date: 2026-05-11

Description:
Metadata adds structured context such as source, author, section, date, and access level. Filters can narrow retrieval before or after vector similarity search.
Hamza Qureshi is coordinating a controlled experiment at Digital Library Unit. The experiment evaluates metadata filtering with Qdrant. The supporting prototype uses JSON for one part of the workflow. The record is deliberately written with repeated entities so that tests can measure whether an embedding model groups related passages and whether metadata extraction can identify people, organizations, places, dates, and technical terms.

Procedure:
1. Load the record without changing its original wording.
2. Split the document using the chunking strategy under evaluation.
3. Attach the record code, title, owner, and date to every resulting chunk.
4. Generate embeddings using the configured embedding model.
5. Store vectors and metadata in the selected vector database.
6. Search using both a direct keyword query and a paraphrased query.
7. Compare the returned record codes with the expected relevant records.

Expected observations:
A query about metadata filtering should retrieve this record or another relevant record. A query containing the name Hamza Qureshi may retrieve records owned by the same person. A query about Peshawar should be supported by location metadata. Exact searches for Qdrant and JSON should remain possible even when semantic similarity is used.

Quality checks:
- No text should disappear during file loading.
- Chunk boundaries should not break every sentence into isolated fragments.
- Each chunk should retain a stable source record code.
- Similarity scores should be stored with the retrieved chunk identifiers.
- The system should not treat fictional examples as verified real-world facts.

End of record.
